Germany\") | 80331–81929 |\n| [Dialling codes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_dialling_codes_in_Germany \"List of dialling codes in Germany\") | 089 |\n| [Vehicle registration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle_registration_plates_of_Germany \"Vehicle registration plates of Germany\") | M, MUC |\n| Website | [stadt.muenchen.de](https://stadt.muenchen.de/en/rathaus.html) |\n\n**Munich**[[a]](#cite_note-3) ([German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language \"German language\"): *München* [[ˈmʏnçn̩]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Standard_German \"Help:IPA/Standard German\") [ⓘ](/wiki/File:Muenchentitle.ogg \"File:Muenchentitle.ogg\"), [Bavarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bavarian_language \"Bavarian language\"): *Minga* [[ˈmɪŋ(ː)ɐ]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA \"Help:IPA\") [ⓘ](/wiki/File:Bar-M%C3%BCnchen.ogg \"File:Bar-München.ogg\")) is the capital and most populous city of the German state of [Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bavaria \"Bavaria\"). As of 30 November 2024, its population was 1,604,384,[[3]](#cite_note-stadtbevoelkerung-4) making it the [third most-populous city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Germany_by_population \"List of cities in Germany by population\") in Germany after [Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin \"Berlin\") and [Hamburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamburg \"Hamburg\"). Munich is the largest city in Germany that is not [a state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_of_Germany \"States of Germany\") of [its own](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City-state \"City-state\"), and it ranks as the 11th-largest city in the [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union \"European Union\") (EU). The metropolitan area has around 3 million inhabitants, and the broader [Munich Metropolitan Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_Metropolitan_Region \"Munich Metropolitan Region\") is home to about 6.2 million people.[[4]](#cite_note-mmr_official_site-5) It is the [third largest metropolitan region by GDP in the EU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_EU_metropolitan_regions_by_GDP#2021_ranking_of_top_four_German_metropolitan_regions \"List of EU metropolitan regions by GDP\"). Munich is located on the river [Isar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isar \"Isar\") north of the [Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alps \"Alps\"). It is the seat of the [Upper Bavarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Bavaria \"Upper Bavaria\") administrative region. With 4,800 people per km2, Munich is Germany's most densely populated municipality. It is also the second-largest city in the [Bavarian dialect area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bavarian_language \"Bavarian language\") after [Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vienna \"Vienna\").\n\nThe first record of Munich dates to 1158. The city has played an important role in Bavarian and German history. During the [Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation \"Reformation\"), it remained a Catholic stronghold. Munich became the capital of the [Kingdom of Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Bavaria \"Kingdom of Bavaria\") in 1806 and developed as a centre for arts, architecture, culture, and science. The [House of Wittelsbach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Wittelsbach \"House of Wittelsbach\") ruled until 1918, when the [German revolution of 1918–1919](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_revolution_of_1918–1919 \"German revolution of 1918–1919\") ended their reign and saw the short-lived [Bavarian Soviet Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bavarian_Soviet_Republic \"Bavarian Soviet Republic\"). In the 1920s, Munich became a centre of political movements, including the rise of the [Nazi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Party \"Nazi Party\"). The city was known as the \"Capital of the Movement\". During [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II \"World War II\"), Munich was heavily bombed, but much of its historic architecture has since been restored.[[5]](#cite_note-englund-6)[[6]](#cite_note-zensus-7) After the war, the city's population and economy grew rapidly. Munich hosted the [1972 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_Summer_Olympics \"1972 Summer Olympics\") and the [1974 FIFA World Cup final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_FIFA_World_Cup_final \"1974 FIFA World Cup final\").\n\nMunich is a major centre for science, technology, finance, innovation, business, and tourism. It has a high standard of living, ranking first in Germany and third worldwide in the 2018 [Mercer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercer_(consulting_firm) \"Mercer (consulting firm)\") survey.[[7]](#cite_note-mercer-8) It was named the world's most liveable city by [*Monocle*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monocle_(brand) \"Monocle (brand)\")'s [Quality of Life Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_quality_of_life_indices \"City quality of life indices\") 2018.[[8]](#cite_note-forbes-9) Munich is the wealthiest city in the EU by GDP per capita among cities with over one million inhabitants[[9]](#cite_note-10) and is among the most expensive German cities for real estate and rents.[[10]](#cite_note-biimmobilien-11)[[11]](#cite_note-hausimmobilien-12) In 2023, 30.1% of residents were foreigners, and 19.4% were German citizens with a [migration background](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migration_background \"Migration background\") from abroad.[[12]](#cite_note-migr2023-13) Munich's economy is based on [high tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_tech \"High tech\"), automobiles, the service sector, information technology, biotechnology, engineering, and electronics. Multinational companies such as [BMW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BMW \"BMW\"), [Siemens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siemens \"Siemens\"), [Allianz SE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allianz_SE \"Allianz SE\"), and [Munich Re](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_Re \"Munich Re\") are headquartered there. The city has two research universities and many scientific institutions.[[13]](#cite_note-nature-14) Munich is known for its architecture, cultural venues, sports events, exhibitions, and the annual [Oktoberfest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oktoberfest \"Oktoberfest\"), the world's largest *[Volksfest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volksfest \"Volksfest\")*.[[14]](#cite_note-tourismus-15)\n\n## History\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=1 \"Edit section: History\")]\n\nMain article: [History of Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Munich \"History of Munich\")\n\nFor a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Munich \"Timeline of Munich\").\n\n### Etymology\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=2 \"Edit section: Etymology\")]\n\nMunich was a tiny 10th-century [monastic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasticism \"Monasticism\") settlement, which was named *zu den Munchen* (\"to the monks\"). The Old High German *Munche* served as the base for the modern German city name *München*.[[15]](#cite_note-16)\n\n### Early history\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=3 \"Edit section: Early history\")]\n\nThe river [Isar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isar \"Isar\") was a prehistoric [trade route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_route \"Trade route\") and in the [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age \"Bronze Age\") Munich was among the largest [raft ports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raft \"Raft\") in Europe.[[16]](#cite_note-Bernan_Associates_UNESCO-17) Bronze Age settlements up to four millennia old have been discovered.[[17]](#cite_note-18) Evidence of [Celtic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celt \"Celt\") settlements from the [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age \"Iron Age\") have been discovered in areas around [Ramersdorf-Perlach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramersdorf-Perlach \"Ramersdorf-Perlach\").[[18]](#cite_note-19)\n\nThe ancient Roman road Via Julia, which connected [Augsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augsburg \"Augsburg\") and [Salzburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salzburg \"Salzburg\"), crossed over the Isar south of Munich, at the towns of [Baierbrunn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baierbrunn \"Baierbrunn\") and [Gauting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauting \"Gauting\").[[19]](#cite_note-20) A Roman settlement north-east of Munich was excavated in the neighborhood of [Denning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denning_(Munich) \"Denning (Munich)\").[[20]](#cite_note-21)\n\nStarting in the 6th century, the [Baiuvarii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baiuvarii \"Baiuvarii\") populated the area around what is now modern Munich, such as in [Johanneskirchen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johanneskirchen \"Johanneskirchen\"), [Feldmoching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feldmoching \"Feldmoching\"), [Bogenhausen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bogenhausen \"Bogenhausen\") and [Pasing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasing \"Pasing\").[[21]](#cite_note-22)[[22]](#cite_note-23) The first known Christian church was built ca. 815 in Fröttmanning.[[23]](#cite_note-24)\n\n### Middle Ages\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=4 \"Edit section: Middle Ages\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Nuremberg_Chronicle_f_225v_226r.jpg/330px-Nuremberg_Chronicle_f_225v_226r.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nuremberg_Chronicle_f_225v_226r.jpg)\n\nMunich in a 1493 woodcut from [Hartmann Schedel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hartmann_Schedel \"Hartmann Schedel\")'s *[Nuremberg Chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuremberg_Chronicle \"Nuremberg Chronicle\")*\n\nThe first medieval bridges across the river Isar were located in current city areas of Munich and [Landshut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landshut \"Landshut\").[[16]](#cite_note-Bernan_Associates_UNESCO-17) [Henry the Lion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_the_Lion \"Henry the Lion\"), the Duke of Saxony and Bavaria, founded the town of Munich in his territory to control the [salt trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_trade \"Salt trade\"), after having burned down the town of Föhring and its bridges over the Isar.[[24]](#cite_note-25) Historians date this event at about 1158.[[25]](#cite_note-freed-26): 167\n\nHenry built a new toll bridge, customs house and a coin market closer to his home, somewhat upstream, at a settlement around the area of modern old town Munich. This new toll bridge most likely crossed the Isar where the Museuminsel and the modern Ludwigsbrücke are now located.[[26]](#cite_note-27)\n\n[Otto of Freising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_of_Freising \"Otto of Freising\") protested to his nephew, [Emperor Frederick Barbarossa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Barbarossa \"Frederick Barbarossa\"). However, on 14 June 1158, in [Augsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augsburg \"Augsburg\"), the conflict was settled in favor of Duke Henry. The *[Augsburg Arbitration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augsburg_Arbitration \"Augsburg Arbitration\")* mentions the name of the location in dispute as *forum apud Munichen*. Although Bishop Otto had lost his bridge, the arbiters ordered Duke Henry to pay a third of his income to the Bishop in Freising as compensation.[[27]](#cite_note-28)[[28]](#cite_note-29)[[29]](#cite_note-30)\n\n14 June 1158 is considered the official founding day of the city of Munich. Archaeological excavations at Marienhof Square (near [Marienplatz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marienplatz \"Marienplatz\")) in advance of the expansion of the S-Bahn (subway) in 2012 discovered shards of vessels from the 11th century, which prove again that the settlement of Munich must be older than the Augsburg Arbitration of 1158.[[30]](#cite_note-31)[[31]](#cite_note-32) The old [St. Peter's Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Peter's_Church,_Munich \"St. Peter's Church, Munich\") near Marienplatz is also believed to predate the founding date of the town.[[32]](#cite_note-33)\n\nIn 1175, Munich received city status and fortification. In 1180, after Henry the Lion's fall from grace with Emperor Frederick Barbarossa, including his trial and exile, [Otto I Wittelsbach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_I_of_Wittelsbach,_Duke_of_Bavaria \"Otto I of Wittelsbach, Duke of Bavaria\") became Duke of Bavaria, and Munich was handed to the [Bishop of Freising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Munich_and_Freising \"Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Munich and Freising\"). In 1240, Munich was transferred to [Otto II Wittelsbach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_II_Wittelsbach,_Duke_of_Bavaria \"Otto II Wittelsbach, Duke of Bavaria\") and in 1255, when the [Duchy of Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Bavaria \"Duchy of Bavaria\") was split in two, Munich became the ducal residence of [Upper Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Bavaria \"Upper Bavaria\").\n\nDuke [Louis IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_IV,_Holy_Roman_Emperor \"Louis IV, Holy Roman Emperor\"), a native of Munich, was elected [German king](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_king \"German king\") in 1314 and crowned as [Holy Roman Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor \"Holy Roman Emperor\") in 1328.[[33]](#cite_note-muenchen-stadtgeschichte-34) He strengthened the city's position by granting it the salt monopoly with [staple rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stapelrecht \"Stapelrecht\"), thus assuring it of additional income.[[34]](#cite_note-35)\n\nThe growth of Munich was aided by its location on a gravel plain, where the Isar branched into multiple streams that in turn provided drinking water, defensive advantages, and power for many mills and industries within the city.[[35]](#cite_note-36)\n\nIn the 15th century, Munich underwent a revival of [Gothic arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_art \"Gothic art\"): the Old Town Hall was enlarged, and Munich's largest [Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_architecture \"Gothic architecture\") church – the [Frauenkirche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_Frauenkirche \"Munich Frauenkirche\") – now a cathedral, was constructed in only 20 years, starting in 1468.\n\n### Capital of reunited Bavaria\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=5 \"Edit section: Capital of reunited Bavaria\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Antiquarium%2C_M%C3%BCnchner_Residenz.jpg/250px-Antiquarium%2C_M%C3%BCnchner_Residenz.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Antiquarium,_Münchner_Residenz.jpg)\n\nThe Renaissance *Antiquarium* of the [Munich Residenz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_Residenz \"Munich Residenz\")\n\nWhen Bavaria was reunited in 1506 after the [War of the Succession of Landshut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Succession_of_Landshut \"War of the Succession of Landshut\") against the [Duchy of Bavaria-Landshut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Bavaria-Landshut \"Duchy of Bavaria-Landshut\"), Munich became its capital.[[33]](#cite_note-muenchen-stadtgeschichte-34) During the 16th century Munich became a centre of the [Counter-Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-Reformation \"Counter-Reformation\").[[33]](#cite_note-muenchen-stadtgeschichte-34) The [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance \"Renaissance\") movement beset Munich and the Bavarian branch of the [House of Wittelsbach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Wittelsbach \"House of Wittelsbach\") under Duke [Albrecht V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_V,_Duke_of_Bavaria \"Albert V, Duke of Bavaria\") who bolstered their prestige by conjuring up a lineage that reached back to [classical antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_antiquity \"Classical antiquity\"). In 1568 Albrecht V built the Antiquarium to house the [Wittelsbach collection of Greek and Roman antiquities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staatliche_Antikensammlungen \"Staatliche Antikensammlungen\") in the [Munich Residenz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Residenz,_Munich \"Residenz, Munich\").[[36]](#cite_note-37) Albrecht V appointed the composer [Orlando di Lasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando_di_Lasso \"Orlando di Lasso\") as director of the court orchestra and tempted numerous Italian musicians to work at the Munich court, establishing Munich as a hub for late [Renaissance music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_music \"Renaissance music\").[[37]](#cite_note-38) During the rule of [Duke William V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_V,_Duke_of_Bavaria \"William V, Duke of Bavaria\") Munich began to be called the \"German Rome\" and William V began presenting Emperor [Charlemagne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlemagne \"Charlemagne\") as ancestor of the Wittelsbach dynasty.[[38]](#cite_note-39)\n\nDuke William V further cemented the Wittelsbach rule by commissioning the [Jesuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesuit \"Jesuit\") [Michaelskirche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Michael's_Church,_Munich \"St. Michael's Church, Munich\"). He had the sermons of his Jesuit court preacher [Jeremias Drexel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremias_Drexel \"Jeremias Drexel\") translated from Latin into German and published them to a greater audience.[[39]](#cite_note-40) William V was addressed with the epithet \"the Pious\" and like his contemporary Wittelsbach dukes promoted himself as \"father of the land\" (*Landesvater*), encouraged pilgrimages and [Marian devotions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veneration_of_Mary_in_the_Catholic_Church \"Veneration of Mary in the Catholic Church\").[[40]](#cite_note-41) William V had the [Hofbräuhaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hofbräuhaus \"Hofbräuhaus\") built in 1589.[[33]](#cite_note-muenchen-stadtgeschichte-34) It would become the prototype for [beer halls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_hall \"Beer hall\") across Munich.\n\nThe [Catholic League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_League_(German) \"Catholic League (German)\") was founded in Munich in 1609. In 1623, during the [Thirty Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirty_Years'_War \"Thirty Years' War\") (1618–1648), Munich became an electoral residence when Duke [Maximilian I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximilian_I,_Elector_of_Bavaria \"Maximilian I, Elector of Bavaria\") was invested with the [electoral dignity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_dignity \"Electoral dignity\") of the [Holy Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire \"Holy Roman Empire\"), but in 1632 the city was occupied by King [Gustavus Adolphus of Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustavus_Adolphus_of_Sweden \"Gustavus Adolphus of Sweden\").[[41]](#cite_note-42) In 1634, Swedish and Spanish troops advanced on Munich. Maximilian I published a plague ordinance to halt an epidemic escalation.[[42]](#cite_note-43) The [bubonic plague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bubonic_plague \"Bubonic plague\") nevertheless ravaged Munich and the surrounding countryside in 1634 and 1635.[[43]](#cite_note-44) Troops again converged on Munich in 1647 and precautions were taken, so as to avoid another epidemic.[[44]](#cite_note-45) The city remained physically untouched during the war.[[5]](#cite_note-englund-6)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/Marcktzumuenchen.png/250px-Marcktzumuenchen.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Marcktzumuenchen.png)\n\n[Marienplatz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marienplatz \"Marienplatz\"), c. 1650\n\nDuring its time as the capital of the [Electorate of Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electorate_of_Bavaria \"Electorate of Bavaria\"), Munich was an important centre of [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque \"Baroque\") life, but also had to suffer under [Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Habsburg \"House of Habsburg\") occupations in 1704 after Bavaria's defeat in the [War of the Spanish Succession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Spanish_Succession \"War of the Spanish Succession\") and again in 1742 during the [War of the Austrian Succession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Austrian_Succession \"War of the Austrian Succession\").[[33]](#cite_note-muenchen-stadtgeschichte-34) When Elector [Maximilian III Joseph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximilian_III_Joseph \"Maximilian III Joseph\") died in 1777, the succession empowered the [Palatinate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Count_palatine \"Count palatine\") branch within the House of Wittelsbach.[[45]](#cite_note-46) In 1785, Elector [Karl Theodor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Theodore,_Elector_of_Bavaria \"Charles Theodore, Elector of Bavaria\") invited Count Rumford ([Benjamin Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Thompson \"Benjamin Thompson\")) to Munich to implement social reforms, including [workhouses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workhouses \"Workhouses\") for the poor and army restructuring with improved conditions for soldiers.[[46]](#cite_note-47) In the 1790s, Munich became the largest German city to remove its [fortifications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortifications \"Fortifications\"), starting in 1791 under Karl Theodor and Rumford.[[47]](#cite_note-48) After 1793, citizens built new structures outside the former city walls.[[48]](#cite_note-49)\n\nAfter making an alliance with Napoleonic France, the city became the capital of the new [Kingdom of Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Bavaria \"Kingdom of Bavaria\") in 1806 with Elector [Maximilian IV Joseph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximilian_I_Joseph_of_Bavaria \"Maximilian I Joseph of Bavaria\") becoming its first king. The state parliament (the *[Landtag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landtag \"Landtag\")*) and the new [archdiocese of Munich and Freising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Munich_and_Freising \"Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Munich and Freising\") were also located in the city.[[49]](#cite_note-50)\n\nThe establishment of Bavarian state sovereignty made Munich the centre of a modernising kingdom. In 1802, King Maximilian Joseph [secularised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularised \"Secularised\") Bavaria, dissolving [monasteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasteries \"Monasteries\") and selling church lands to generate state revenue. He also took control of the right to brew beer, granting a monopoly to Munich's wealthiest brewers in exchange for substantial taxes. In 1807, he abolished restrictions on the number of brewery workers, allowing brewers to meet growing demand.[[50]](#cite_note-51)\n\nIn October 1810, a beer festival was held on the meadows outside Munich to celebrate the crown prince's wedding to Princess [Therese of Saxe-Hildburghausen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Therese_of_Saxe-Hildburghausen \"Therese of Saxe-Hildburghausen\"). The event, featuring parades in regional dress, later evolved into the annual [Oktoberfest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oktoberfest \"Oktoberfest\"), now held at [Theresienwiese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theresienwiese \"Theresienwiese\").[[51]](#cite_note-52)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Propylaeen_Muenchen-1.jpg/250px-Propylaeen_Muenchen-1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Propylaeen_Muenchen-1.jpg)\n\n[Neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture \"Neoclassical architecture\") buildings erected under [Ludwig I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwig_I \"Ludwig I\"), such as the [Propylaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propylaea_(Munich) \"Propylaea (Munich)\"), earned Munich the nickname 'Athens on the Isar'.\n\nThe first Munich railway station was built in 1839, with a line going to [Augsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augsburg \"Augsburg\") in the west. By 1849 a newer Munich Central Train Station ([München Hauptbahnhof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/München_Hauptbahnhof \"München Hauptbahnhof\")) was completed, with a line going to [Landshut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landshut \"Landshut\") and [Regensburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regensburg \"Regensburg\") in the north.[[52]](#cite_note-53)[[53]](#cite_note-54) In 1825 [Ludwig I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwig_I_of_Bavaria \"Ludwig I of Bavaria\") had ascended to the throne and commissioned leading architects such as [Leo von Klenze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_von_Klenze \"Leo von Klenze\") to design a series of public museums in [neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture \"Neoclassical architecture\") style. Between 1856 and 1861 the court gardener [Carl von Effner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_von_Effner \"Carl von Effner\") landscaped the banks of the river [Isar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isar \"Isar\") and established the *Maximilian Gardens*. Leo von Klenze supervised the construction of the [Propylaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propylaea_(Munich) \"Propylaea (Munich)\") between 1854 and 1862.[[54]](#cite_note-55) In 1857 the construction of the [Maximilianeum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximilianeum \"Maximilianeum\") was begun.[[55]](#cite_note-56) The grand building projects of Ludwig I gave Munich the endearment \"Athens on the Isar\" (German: *Isar-Athen*) and \"Monaco di Bavaria\".[[56]](#cite_note-57)\n\nBy the time [Ludwig II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwig_II_of_Bavaria \"Ludwig II of Bavaria\") became king in 1864, he remained mostly aloof from his capital and focused more on his fanciful castles in the Bavarian countryside. In 1876 Munich hosted the first German Art and Industry Exhibition, which showcased the northern [Neo-Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Renaissance \"Neo-Renaissance\") fashion that came to be the [German Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire \"German Empire\")'s predominant style. Munich based artists put on the German National Applied Arts Exhibition in 1888, showcasing [Baroque Revival architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_Revival_architecture \"Baroque Revival architecture\") and [Rococo Revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rococo_Revival \"Rococo Revival\") designs.[[57]](#cite_note-58)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/M%C3%BCnchen_Jugendstil_1.jpg/250px-M%C3%BCnchen_Jugendstil_1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:München_Jugendstil_1.jpg)\n\n[Jugendstil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jugendstil \"Jugendstil\") style house at Leopoldstr. 77. At the beginning of the 20th century, Munich was a hub for the Jugendstil movement.\n\nThe Prince Regent [Luitpold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luitpold,_Prince_Regent_of_Bavaria \"Luitpold, Prince Regent of Bavaria\")'s reign from 1886 to 1912 was marked by tremendous artistic and cultural activity in Munich.[[58]](#cite_note-59) At the dawn of the 20th century Munich was an epicenter for the [Jugendstil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jugendstil \"Jugendstil\") movement, combining a liberal magazine culture with progressive [industrial design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_design \"Industrial design\") and architecture. The German art movement took its name from the Munich magazine *Die Jugend* (*The Youth*).[[59]](#cite_note-60) Prominent Munich Jugendstil artists include [Hans Eduard von Berlepsch-Valendas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Eduard_von_Berlepsch-Valendas \"Hans Eduard von Berlepsch-Valendas\"), [Otto Eckmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_Eckmann \"Otto Eckmann\"),[[60]](#cite_note-61) [Margarethe von Brauchitsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margarethe_von_Brauchitsch?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Margarethe von Brauchitsch (page does not exist)\") [[de](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margarethe%20von%20Brauchitsch \"de:Margarethe von Brauchitsch\")], [August Endell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_Endell \"August Endell\"), [Hermann Obrist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermann_Obrist \"Hermann Obrist\"), [Wilhelm von Debschitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_von_Debschitz \"Wilhelm von Debschitz\"),[[61]](#cite_note-62) and [Richard Riemerschmid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Riemerschmid \"Richard Riemerschmid\"). In 1905 two large department stores opened in Munich, the Kaufhaus Oberpollinger and the [Warenhaus Hermann Tietz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermann_Tietz \"Hermann Tietz\"), both having been designed by the architect [Max Littmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Littmann \"Max Littmann\").[[62]](#cite_note-63) In 1911 the [expressionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expressionist \"Expressionist\") group [Der Blaue Reiter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Der_Blaue_Reiter \"Der Blaue Reiter\") was established in Munich. Its founding members include [Gabriele Münter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriele_Münter \"Gabriele Münter\").[[63]](#cite_note-64)\n\n### World War I to World War II\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=6 \"Edit section: World War I to World War II\")]\n\nFollowing the outbreak of World War I in 1914, life in Munich became very difficult, as the [Allied blockade of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_of_Germany_(1914–1919) \"Blockade of Germany (1914–1919)\") led to food and fuel shortages. During a French air raid in 1916, three bombs fell on Munich.[[64]](#cite_note-65)\n\nIn 1916, the 'Bayerische Motoren Werke' ([BMW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BMW \"BMW\")) produced its first [aircraft engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_engine \"Aircraft engine\") in Munich.[[65]](#cite_note-66) The public limited company BMW AG was founded in 1918, with [Camillo Castiglioni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camillo_Castiglioni \"Camillo Castiglioni\") owning one third of the share capital. In 1922 BMW relocated its headquarters to a factory in Munich.[[66]](#cite_note-67)\n\nAfter World War I, the city was at the centre of substantial political unrest. In November 1918, on the eve of the German revolution, [Ludwig III of Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwig_III_of_Bavaria \"Ludwig III of Bavaria\") and his family fled the city. After the murder of the first republican [premier of Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Ministers-President_of_Bavaria \"List of Ministers-President of Bavaria\") [Kurt Eisner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurt_Eisner \"Kurt Eisner\") in February 1919 by [Anton Graf von Arco auf Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anton_Graf_von_Arco_auf_Valley \"Anton Graf von Arco auf Valley\"), the [Bavarian Soviet Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bavarian_Soviet_Republic \"Bavarian Soviet Republic\") was proclaimed.[[67]](#cite_note-68) The November 1918 revolution ended the reign of the Wittelsbach in Bavaria.[[68]](#cite_note-City_Halls_and_Civic_Materialism-69) In *[Mein Kampf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mein_Kampf \"Mein Kampf\")* [Adolf Hitler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolf_Hitler \"Adolf Hitler\") described his political activism in Munich after November 1918 as the \"Beginning of My Political Activity\". Hitler called the short-lived Bavarian Soviet Republic \"the rule of the Jews\".[[69]](#cite_note-70)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/Bundesarchiv_Bild_119-1486%2C_Hitler-Putsch%2C_M%C3%BCnchen%2C_Marienplatz.jpg/250px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_119-1486%2C_Hitler-Putsch%2C_M%C3%BCnchen%2C_Marienplatz.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_119-1486,_Hitler-Putsch,_München,_Marienplatz.jpg)\n\nUnrest during the 1923 [Beer Hall Putsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_Hall_Putsch \"Beer Hall Putsch\"), a failed [coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coup_d'état \"Coup d'état\") led by [Adolf Hitler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolf_Hitler \"Adolf Hitler\"), [Erich Ludendorff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erich_Ludendorff \"Erich Ludendorff\"), and other *[Kampfbund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kampfbund \"Kampfbund\")* leaders\n\nIn 1923 [Gustav von Kahr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_von_Kahr \"Gustav von Kahr\") was appointed Bavarian prime minister and immediately planned for the expulsion of all Jews who did not hold German citizenship. Chief of Police [Ernst Pöhner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Pöhner \"Ernst Pöhner\") and [Wilhelm Frick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_Frick \"Wilhelm Frick\") openly indulged in antisemitism, while Bavarian judges praised people on the political right as patriotic for their crimes and handed down mild sentences.[[70]](#cite_note-71) In 1923, Adolf Hitler and his supporters, who were concentrated in Munich, staged the [Beer Hall Putsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_Hall_Putsch \"Beer Hall Putsch\"), an attempt to overthrow the [Weimar Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weimar_Republic \"Weimar Republic\") and seize power. The revolt failed, resulting in Hitler's arrest and the temporary crippling of the [Nazi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Party \"Nazi Party\") (NSDAP).[[71]](#cite_note-72)\n\nThe unofficial city anthem of Munich, recorded in 1929\n\nMunich was chosen as capital for the [Free State of Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_State_of_Bavaria \"Free State of Bavaria\") and acquired increased responsibility for administering the city itself and the surrounding districts. Offices needed to be built for bureaucracy, so a 12-story office building was erected in the southern part of the historic city centre in the late 1920s.[[68]](#cite_note-City_Halls_and_Civic_Materialism-69)\n\nMunich again became important to the Nazis when they took power in Germany in 1933. The party created its first [concentration camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_concentration_camp \"Nazi concentration camp\") at [Dachau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dachau_concentration_camp \"Dachau concentration camp\"), 16 km (10 mi) north-west of the city. Because of its importance to the rise of National Socialism, Munich was referred to as the *Hauptstadt der Bewegung* (\"Capital of the Movement\").[[72]](#cite_note-73)\n\nThe NSDAP headquarters and the documentation apparatus for controlling all aspects of life were located in Munich. Nazi organizations, such as the [National Socialist Women's League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_Women's_League \"National Socialist Women's League\") and the [Gestapo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestapo \"Gestapo\"), had their offices along [Brienner Straße](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brienner_Straße_(Munich) \"Brienner Straße (Munich)\") and around the [Königsplatz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Königsplatz,_Munich \"Königsplatz, Munich\"). The party acquired 68 buildings in the area and many *Führerbauten* (\"*Führer* buildings\") were built to reflect a new aesthetic of power.[[73]](#cite_note-74) Construction work for the [Führerbau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Führerbau \"Führerbau\") and the party headquarters (known as the [Brown House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_House,_Munich \"Brown House, Munich\")) started in September 1933.[[74]](#cite_note-75) The *[Haus der Kunst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haus_der_Kunst \"Haus der Kunst\")* (House of German Art) was the first building to be commissioned by Hitler. The architect [Paul Troost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Troost \"Paul Troost\") was asked to start work shortly after the Nazis had seized power because \"the most German of all German cities\" was left with no exhibition building when in 1931 the [Glass Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glaspalast_(Munich) \"Glaspalast (Munich)\") was destroyed in an arson attack.[[75]](#cite_note-76)\n\nThe city was the site where the 1938 [Munich Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_Agreement \"Munich Agreement\") was signed between [Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Germany \"Nazi Germany\"), the United Kingdom, [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_French_Republic \"Third French Republic\") and [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy \"Kingdom of Italy\").[[76]](#cite_note-77) On 8 November 1939, shortly after the Second World War had begun, [Georg Elser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_Elser \"Georg Elser\") planted a bomb in the [Bürgerbräukeller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bürgerbräukeller \"Bürgerbräukeller\") in Munich in an attempt to assassinate Adolf Hitler, who held a political party speech. Hitler, however, had left the building minutes before the bomb went off.[[77]](#cite_note-78)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Wardamage2.jpg/250px-Wardamage2.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Wardamage2.jpg)\n\nView of Munich city centre in 1945 after the devastation of World War II, and in 1989 after reconstruction, featuring the restored [Old Town Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Town_Hall,_Munich \"Old Town Hall, Munich\"), [Talburgtor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talburgtor \"Talburgtor\"), and [Heilig-Geist-Kirche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heilig-Geist-Kirche,_Munich \"Heilig-Geist-Kirche, Munich\")\n\nDuring the war, Munich was the location of multiple [forced labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_labour_under_German_rule_during_World_War_II \"Forced labour under German rule during World War II\") camps, including two *Polenlager* camps for Polish youth,[[78]](#cite_note-79)[[79]](#cite_note-80) and 40 subcamps of the [Dachau concentration camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dachau_concentration_camp \"Dachau concentration camp\"), in which men and women of various nationalities were held.[[80]](#cite_note-81) With up to 17,000 prisoners in 1945, the largest subcamp of Dachau was the [Munich-Allach concentration camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich-Allach_concentration_camp \"Munich-Allach concentration camp\"). By mid 1942 the majority of Jews living in Munich and the suburbs had been deported.[[81]](#cite_note-82)\n\nMunich was the base of the [White Rose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Rose \"White Rose\"), a student [resistance movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resistance_movement \"Resistance movement\"). The core members were arrested and executed after [Sophie Scholl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sophie_Scholl \"Sophie Scholl\") and her brother [Hans Scholl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Scholl \"Hans Scholl\") were caught distributing leaflets on the campus of the [Ludwig-Maximilians-Universität München](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwig-Maximilians-Universität_München \"Ludwig-Maximilians-Universität München\"), calling upon the youth to rise against Hitler.[[82]](#cite_note-83)\n\nAround 50% of city were heavily damaged by the [bombing of Munich in World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombing_of_Munich_in_World_War_II \"Bombing of Munich in World War II\"), with 71 air raids over five years. After the war, large reconstruction projects restored most of the historically relevant buildings in the old town, that had suffered damage. US troops captured Munich on 30 April 1945.[[83]](#cite_note-84)\n\n### Postwar\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=7 \"Edit section: Postwar\")]\n\nIn the aftermath of World War II, Germany was subject to [US Military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Military \"US Military\") occupation.[[84]](#cite_note-85) Due to Polish annexation of the [former eastern territories of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Former_eastern_territories_of_Germany \"Former eastern territories of Germany\") and [expulsion of Germans from all over Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_and_expulsion_of_Germans_(1944–1950) \"Flight and expulsion of Germans (1944–1950)\"), Munich operated over a thousand refugee camps for 151,113 people in October 1946.[[85]](#cite_note-86) After US occupation Munich was completely rebuilt following a meticulous plan, which preserved its pre-war street grid, bar a few exceptions owing to then-modern traffic concepts. In 1957, Munich's population surpassed one million. The city continued to play a highly significant role in the [West German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_German \"West German\") economy, politics and culture, giving rise to its nickname *Heimliche Hauptstadt* (\"secret capital\") in the decades after World War II.[[86]](#cite_note-87)\n\nThe [Free State of Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_State_of_Bavaria \"Free State of Bavaria\") used the [arms industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arms_industry \"Arms industry\") as kernel for its [high tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_tech \"High tech\") development policy.[[87]](#cite_note-Making_Competitive_Cities-88) Since 1963, Munich has been hosting the [Munich Security Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_Security_Conference \"Munich Security Conference\"), a conference on international [security policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Security_policy \"Security policy\") held annually in the [Hotel Bayerischer Hof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotel_Bayerischer_Hof,_Munich \"Hotel Bayerischer Hof, Munich\").[[88]](#cite_note-89) Munich also became known on the political level due to the strong influence of Bavarian politician [Franz Josef Strauss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franz_Josef_Strauss \"Franz Josef Strauss\") from the 1960s to the 1980s. The [Munich Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_Airport \"Munich Airport\"), which commenced operations in 1992, was named in his honor.[[89]](#cite_note-90)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/View_of_the_Olympic_Stadium_from_Olympic_Tower%2C_Munich_-_2018.jpg/250px-View_of_the_Olympic_Stadium_from_Olympic_Tower%2C_Munich_-_2018.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:View_of_the_Olympic_Stadium_from_Olympic_Tower,_Munich_-_2018.jpg)\n\n[Olympiapark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympiapark \"Olympiapark\") was the main venue for the [1972 Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_Olympic_Games \"1972 Olympic Games\") and also hosted the [1974 FIFA World Cup final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_FIFA_World_Cup_final \"1974 FIFA World Cup final\").\n\nMunich hosted the [1972 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_Summer_Olympics \"1972 Summer Olympics\"). After winning the bid in 1966 the [Mayor of Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Munich \"Mayor of Munich\"), [Hans-Jochen Vogel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans-Jochen_Vogel \"Hans-Jochen Vogel\"), accelerated the construction of the [U-Bahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_U-Bahn \"Munich U-Bahn\") subway and the [S-Bahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_S-Bahn \"Munich S-Bahn\") metropolitan commuter railway. In May 1967 the construction work began for a new U-Bahn line connecting the city with the [Olympic Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympiapark_(Munich) \"Olympiapark (Munich)\") (*Olympiapark*). The Olympic Park subway station was built near the [BMW Headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BMW_Headquarters \"BMW Headquarters\") and the line was completed in May 1972, three months before the opening of the 1972 Summer Olympics. Shortly before the opening ceremony, Munich also inaugurated a sizable pedestrian priority zone between [Karlsplatz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karlsplatz_(Stachus) \"Karlsplatz (Stachus)\") and [Marienplatz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marienplatz \"Marienplatz\").[[90]](#cite_note-91) In 1970 the Munich city council released funds so that the [gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_architecture \"Gothic architecture\") facade and [Glockenspiel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rathaus-Glockenspiel \"Rathaus-Glockenspiel\") of the [New City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neues_Rathaus_(Munich) \"Neues Rathaus (Munich)\") (*Neues Rathaus*) could be restored.[[91]](#cite_note-92) During the 1972 Summer Olympics 11 Israeli athletes were murdered by Palestinian terrorists in the [Munich massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_massacre \"Munich massacre\"), when gunmen from the Palestinian \"[Black September](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_September_(group) \"Black September (group)\")\" group took hostage members of the Israeli Olympic team.[[92]](#cite_note-93) In 1974, the [FIFA World Cup final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_FIFA_World_Cup_final \"1974 FIFA World Cup final\") was held at the [Olympic Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_Stadium,_Munich \"Olympic Stadium, Munich\").\n\nMunich and its [urban sprawl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_sprawl \"Urban sprawl\") emerged as the leading German high tech region during the 1980s and 1990s. The urban economy of Munich became characterized by a dynamic labour market, low unemployment, a growing [service economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_economy \"Service economy\") and high per capita income.[[87]](#cite_note-Making_Competitive_Cities-88)\n\nMunich was one of the host cities for the [2006 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_FIFA_World_Cup \"2006 FIFA World Cup\") and both the [UEFA Euro 2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2020 \"UEFA Euro 2020\") – postponed by a year because of the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic \"COVID-19 pandemic\") – and [UEFA Euro 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2024 \"UEFA Euro 2024\").[[93]](#cite_note-94)\n\n## Geography\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=8 \"Edit section: Geography\")]\n\n### Topography\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=9 \"Edit section: Topography\")]\n\nMunich lies on the elevated plains of [Upper Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Bavaria \"Upper Bavaria\") in the Northern [Alpine Foreland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foothills \"Foothills\"), about 50 km north of the [Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alps \"Alps\") and at about 520 m [above mean sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Above_mean_sea_level \"Above mean sea level\"). The rivers [Isar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isar \"Isar\") and [Würm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Würm_(Amper) \"Würm (Amper)\") flow through the city.\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Luftbild_M%C3%BCnchen_Innenstadt.jpg/250px-Luftbild_M%C3%BCnchen_Innenstadt.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Luftbild_München_Innenstadt.jpg)\n\nAerial view of Munich city centre (facing southeast)\n\nThe northern part of this sandy plateau includes a highly fertile [flint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flint \"Flint\") area no longer affected by Alpine tectonic processes, while the southern part is covered with [morainic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moraine \"Moraine\") hills. Between them are [fluvio-glacial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluvio-glacial \"Fluvio-glacial\") outwash plains around Munich. Where these deposits become thinner, [groundwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groundwater \"Groundwater\") can rise through the gravel surface, causing marsh formation in northern Munich.\n\n### Climate\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=10 \"Edit section: Climate\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/M%C3%BCnchen_und_die_Alpen_bei_F%C3%B6hn.jpg/250px-M%C3%BCnchen_und_die_Alpen_bei_F%C3%B6hn.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:München_und_die_Alpen_bei_Föhn.jpg)\n\nView of Munich with the [Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alps \"Alps\") in the background\n\nAccording to the [Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Köppen_climate_classification \"Köppen climate classification\"), the climate is [oceanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate \"Oceanic climate\") (*Cfb*) bordering on [humid continental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate \"Humid continental climate\") (*Dfb*), with features like warm to hot summers and cold winters, but without permanent snow cover.[[94]](#cite_note-95)[[95]](#cite_note-96) The city centre lies between both climates, while the [airport of Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_Airport \"Munich Airport\") has a [humid continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate \"Humid continental climate\"). The warmest month, on average, is July. The coolest is January.\n\nThe proximity to the [Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alps \"Alps\") brings higher volumes of rainfall and consequently greater susceptibility to [flood problems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_flooding \"Urban flooding\"). Studies of [adaptation to climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_adaptation \"Climate change adaptation\") and extreme events are carried out; one of them is the Isar Plan of the [EU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union \"European Union\") Adaptation Climate.[[96]](#cite_note-97)\n\nShowers and thunderstorms bring the highest average monthly precipitation in late spring and throughout the summer. The most precipitation occurs in July, on average. Winter tends to have less precipitation, the least in February.\n\nThe higher elevation and proximity to the Alps cause the city to have more rain and snow than many other parts of Germany. The Alps affect the city's climate in other ways too; for example, the warm downhill wind from the Alps ([föhn wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Föhn_wind \"Föhn wind\")), which can raise temperatures sharply within a few hours even in the winter.\n\nBeing at the centre of Europe, Munich is subject to many climatic influences, so that weather conditions there are more variable than in other European cities, especially those further west and south of the Alps.\n\nMunich is near the Alps. Annual variation in temperature can be significant, because there are no large bodies of water nearby. The winter in Munich is generally cold and overcast, and some Munich winters have significant snow. January is the coldest month. While winter averages remain only moderately cold, and relatively mild for an elevated inland location of Munich's latitude, [inversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inversion_(meteorology) \"Inversion (meteorology)\") from the nearby Alps causes cold air to sink and result in temperatures below −15 °C (5 °F). In Munich the summer is usually pleasantly warm, with daytime temperatures averaging 25 °C (77 °F).\n\nMunich is subject to active [convective](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convective \"Convective\") seasons and sometimes damaging events. The Alpine [thunderstorm system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thunderstorm \"Thunderstorm\") moves along the mountain range, or detaches, heading east-north-east over the foothills of the Alps.[[97]](#cite_note-98)\n\nAt Munich's official [weather stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weather_station \"Weather station\"), the highest and lowest temperatures ever measured are 37.6 °C (100 °F), on 27 June 2026 in Munich City, and −31.6 °C (−24.9 °F), on 12 February 1929 in the Botanic Garden of the city.[[98]](#cite_note-99)[[99]](#cite_note-100)\n\n| Climate data for Munich [DWD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutscher_Wetterdienst \"Deutscher Wetterdienst\")[[101]](#cite_note-102) SKlima.de[[102]](#cite_note-103) Infoclimat[[103]](#cite_note-104) |\n\n#### Climate change\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=11 \"Edit section: Climate change\")]\n\nIn Munich, the general trend of [global warming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_warming \"Global warming\") with a rise of medium yearly temperatures of about 1 °C (1.8 °F) in Germany between 1900 and 2020 can be observed as well. In November 2016 the city council concluded officially that a further rise in medium temperature, a higher number of heat extremes, a rise in the number of hot days and nights with temperatures higher than 20 °C ([tropical nights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_night \"Tropical night\")), a change in [precipitation patterns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precipitation \"Precipitation\"), as well as a rise in the number of local instances of heavy rain, is to be expected as part of the ongoing climate change. The city administration decided to support a joint study from its own Referat für Gesundheit und Umwelt (department for health and environmental issues) and the [German Meteorological Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutscher_Wetterdienst \"Deutscher Wetterdienst\") that will gather data on local weather. The data is supposed to be used to create a plan for action for adapting the city to better deal with climate change as well as an integrated action program for climate protection in Munich. With the help of those programs issues regarding [spatial planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spatial_planning \"Spatial planning\") and settlement density, the development of buildings and green spaces as well as plans for functioning [ventilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ventilation_(architecture) \"Ventilation (architecture)\") in a cityscape can be monitored and managed.[[104]](#cite_note-105)\n\n## Demographics\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=12 \"Edit section: Demographics\")]\n\nMain article: [Demographics of Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Munich \"Demographics of Munich\")\n\nHistorical population\n\n| Year | Pop. | ±% |\n| 1500 | 13,447 | — |\n| 1600 | 21,943 | +63.2% |\n| 1750 | 32,000 | +45.8% |\n| 1880 | 230,023 | +618.8% |\n| 1890 | 349,024 | +51.7% |\n| 1900 | 499,932 | +43.2% |\n| 1910 | 596,467 | +19.3% |\n| 1920 | 666,000 | +11.7% |\n| 1930 | 728,900 | +9.4% |\n| 1940 | 834,500 | +14.5% |\n| 1950 | 823,892 | −1.3% |\n| 1960 | 1,055,457 | +28.1% |\n| 1970 | 1,311,978 | +24.3% |\n| 1980 | 1,298,941 | −1.0% |\n| 1990 | 1,229,026 | −5.4% |\n| 2001 | 1,227,958 | −0.1% |\n| 2011 | 1,348,335 | +9.8% |\n| 2022 | 1,478,638 | +9.7% |\n| Population size may be affected by changes in administrative divisions. |\n\nFrom only 24,000 inhabitants in 1700, the city population doubled about every 30 years. It was 100,000 in 1852, 250,000 in 1883 and 500,000 in 1901. Since then, Munich has become Germany's third-largest city. In 1933, 840,901 inhabitants were counted, and in 1957 over 1 million. Munich has reached 1.5 million in 2022.\n\n### Immigration\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=13 \"Edit section: Immigration\")]\n\nIn December 2023, Munich had 1.58 million inhabitants; 477,855 foreign nationals resided in the city as of 31 December 2023 with 42.88% of these residents being citizens of EU member states, and 29.66% citizens in European states not in the EU (including Kosovo and Turkey).[[105]](#cite_note-106) Along with the Turks, the Croats are one of the two largest foreign minorities in the city, which is why some Croats refer to Munich as their \"second capital\".[[106]](#cite_note-107) The largest groups of foreign nationals were [Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_people \"Turkish people\") (38,947), [Croats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croats \"Croats\") (37,541), [Italians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italians \"Italians\") (28,142), [Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks \"Greeks\") (24,843), [Bosnians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnians \"Bosnians\") (24,161), [Ukrainians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainians \"Ukrainians\") (21,899), and [Austrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrians \"Austrians\") (21,944).\n\n### Religion\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=14 \"Edit section: Religion\")]\n\nAbout 45% of Munich's residents are not affiliated with any religious group; this ratio represents the fastest growing segment of the population. As in the rest of Germany, the Catholic and Protestant churches have experienced a continuous decline in membership. As of 31 December 2017, 31.8% of the city's inhabitants were Catholic, 11.4% Protestant, 0.3% Jewish (see: [History of the Jews in Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Munich \"History of the Jews in Munich\")),[[107]](#cite_note-108) and 3.6% were members of an Orthodox Church ([Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church \"Eastern Orthodox Church\") or [Oriental Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodoxy \"Oriental Orthodoxy\")).[[108]](#cite_note-109) About 1% adhere to other Christian denominations. There is also a small [Old Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Catholic \"Old Catholic\") parish and an English-speaking parish of the [Episcopal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_Church_(United_States) \"Episcopal Church (United States)\") in the city. According to Munich Statistical Office, in 2013 about 6.9% of Munich's population was Muslim.[[109]](#cite_note-110) Munich has the largest [Uyghur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghurs \"Uyghurs\") population with about 800 (whole Germany about 1,600) people with Uyghur diaspora. Many of them fled to Munich due to the Chinese government and are exiled in Munich. Munich is also home to [World Uyghur Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Uyghur_Congress \"World Uyghur Congress\"), which is an [international organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_organisation \"International organisation\") of exiled Uyghurs.[[110]](#cite_note-111)\n\n## Government and politics\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=15 \"Edit section: Government and politics\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/Bayerische_Staatskanzlei_Munich_2014_02.jpg/250px-Bayerische_Staatskanzlei_Munich_2014_02.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bayerische_Staatskanzlei_Munich_2014_02.jpg)\n\nThe [Bavarian State Chancellery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bavarian_State_Chancellery \"Bavarian State Chancellery\") serves as the executive office of the [Minister-President of Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister-President_of_Bavaria \"Minister-President of Bavaria\").\n\nAs the capital of Bavaria, Munich is an important political centre for both the state and country as a whole. It is the seat of the [Landtag of Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landtag_of_Bavaria \"Landtag of Bavaria\"), the [State Chancellery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayerische_Staatskanzlei \"Bayerische Staatskanzlei\"), and all state departments. Several national and international authorities are located in Munich, including the [Federal Finance Court of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Finance_Court_of_Germany \"Federal Finance Court of Germany\"), the [German Patent Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Patent_Office \"German Patent Office\") and the [European Patent Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Patent_Office \"European Patent Office\"). Bavaria has been dominated by the [Christian Social Union in Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Social_Union_in_Bavaria \"Christian Social Union in Bavaria\") (CSU) on a federal, state, and local level since the establishment of the Federal Republic in 1949.\n\n### Mayor\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=16 \"Edit section: Mayor\")]\n\nThe current mayor of Munich is [Dominik Krause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominik_Krause \"Dominik Krause\") of [the Greens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_90/The_Greens \"Alliance 90/The Greens\").\n\nThe governing coalition after the 2020 election consisted of the Greens and the SPD, as well as the allied Volt and Pink List, which had one seat each. While the Greens had gained the most seats in the city council, the mayor was from the SPD. The parties made an agreement including social and ecological goals and focusing on heightening stipulations for new development in the city. The Red-Green alliance is a reprise of the 1990–2014 coalition, which was interrupted by a six-year CSU-SPD majority from 2014 to 2020.[[112]](#cite_note-p829-114)[[113]](#cite_note-x533-115)\n\n### State Landtag\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=18 \"Edit section: State Landtag\")]\n\nMunich is represented in the [Landtag of Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landtag_of_Bavaria \"Landtag of Bavaria\") by nine constituencies. Blume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Markus_Blume \"Markus Blume\") |\n| 108 München-Schwabing | * Schwabing-Freimann, Maxvorstadt, Altstadt-Lehe | [GRÜNE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_90/The_Greens \"Alliance 90/The Greens\") | Christian Hierneis |\n| 109 München-Mitte | * Ludwigsvorstadt-Isarvorstadt, Schwanthalerhöhe * Parts of Au-Haidhausen and Untergiesing-Harlaching | [GRÜNE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_90/The_Greens \"Alliance 90/The Greens\") | [Ludwig Hartmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwig_Hartmann \"Ludwig Hartmann\") |\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/The_Maximilianeum_Building_2012.jpg/250px-The_Maximilianeum_Building_2012.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_Maximilianeum_Building_2012.jpg)\n\nThe [Maximilianeum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximilianeum \"Maximilianeum\") is the seat of the [Bavarian State Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bavarian_State_Parliament \"Bavarian State Parliament\").\n\n### Federal parliament\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=19 \"Edit section: Federal parliament\")]\n\nMunich is represented in the [Bundestag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bundestag \"Bundestag\") by four constituencies. In the [20th Bundestag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_members_of_the_20th_Bundestag \"List of members of the 20th Bundestag\"), they were represented as follows:\n\n| Constituency | Districts | Representative | Party |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| 217 [Munich North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_North_(electoral_district) \"Munich North (electoral district)\") | Maxvorstadt, Schwabing-West, Moosach, Milbertshofen-Am Hart, Schwabing-Freimann, Feldmoching-Hasenbergl | [Bernhard Loos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernhard_Loos \"Bernhard Loos\") | [CSU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Social_Union_in_Bavaria \"Christian Social Union in Bavaria\") |\n| 218 [Munich East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_East_(electoral_district) \"Munich East (electoral district)\") | Altstadt-Lehel, Au-Haidhausen, Bogenhausen, Berg am Laim, Trudering-Riem, Ramersdorf-Perlach | [Wolfgang Stefinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolfgang_Stefinger \"Wolfgang Stefinger\") | [CSU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Social_Union_in_Bavaria \"Christian Social Union in Bavaria\") |\n| 219 [Munich South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_South_(electoral_district) \"Munich South (electoral district)\") | Sendling, Sendling-Westpark, Obergiesing, Untergiesing-Harlaching, West/Centre (electoral district)\") | Ludwigsvorstadt-Isarvorstadt, Schwanthalerhöhe, Neuhausen-Nymphenburg, Pasing-Obermenzing, Aubing-Lochhausen-Langwied, Allach-Untermenzing, Laim | [Stephan Pilsinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephan_Pilsinger \"Stephan Pilsinger\") | [CSU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Social_Union_in_Bavaria \"Christian Social Union in Bavaria\") |\n\n### Subdivisions\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=20 \"Edit section: Subdivisions\")]\n\nMain article: [Boroughs of Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boroughs_of_Munich \"Boroughs of Munich\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Stadtbezirke_Lage_in_M%C3%BCnchen.png/250px-Stadtbezirke_Lage_in_M%C3%BCnchen.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Stadtbezirke_Lage_in_München.png)\n\nMunich's boroughs\n\nSince the reform of 1992, Munich is divided into 25 administrative [boroughs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borough \"Borough\") (*Stadtbezirke*). They are subdivided into 105 statistical areas.\n\n[Allach-Untermenzing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allach-Untermenzing \"Allach-Untermenzing\") (23), [Altstadt-Lehel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altstadt-Lehel \"Altstadt-Lehel\") (1), [Aubing-Lochhausen-Langwied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aubing-Lochhausen-Langwied \"Aubing-Lochhausen-Langwied\") (22), [Au-Haidhausen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Au-Haidhausen \"Au-Haidhausen\") (5), [Berg am Laim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berg_am_Laim \"Berg am Laim\") (14), [Bogenhausen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bogenhausen \"Bogenhausen\") (13), [Feldmoching-Hasenbergl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feldmoching-Hasenbergl \"Feldmoching-Hasenbergl\") (24), [Hadern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadern \"Hadern\") (20), [Laim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laim \"Laim\") (25), [Ludwigsvorstadt-Isarvorstadt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwigsvorstadt-Isarvorstadt \"Ludwigsvorstadt-Isarvorstadt\") (2), [Maxvorstadt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxvorstadt \"Maxvorstadt\") (3), [Milbertshofen-Am Hart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milbertshofen-Am_Hart \"Milbertshofen-Am Hart\") (11), [Moosach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moosach_(District_of_Munich) \"Moosach (District of Munich)\") (10), [Neuhausen-Nymphenburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuhausen-Nymphenburg \"Neuhausen-Nymphenburg\") (9), [Obergiesing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergiesing \"Obergiesing\") (17), [Pasing-Obermenzing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasing-Obermenzing \"Pasing-Obermenzing\") (21), [Ramersdorf-Perlach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramersdorf-Perlach \"Ramersdorf-Perlach\") (16), [Schwabing-Freimann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schwabing-Freimann \"Schwabing-Freimann\") (12), [Schwabing-West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schwabing-West \"Schwabing-West\") (4), [Schwanthalerhöhe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schwanthalerhöhe \"Schwanthalerhöhe\") (8), [Sendling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sendling \"Sendling\") (6), [Sendling-Westpark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sendling-Westpark \"Sendling-Westpark\") (7), [Thalkirchen-Obersendling-Forstenried-Fürstenried-Solln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thalkirchen-Obersendling-Forstenried-Fürstenried-Solln \"Thalkirchen-Obersendling-Forstenried-Fürstenried-Solln\") (19), [Trudering-Riem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trudering-Riem \"Trudering-Riem\") (15), and [Untergiesing-Harlaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Untergiesing-Harlaching \"Untergiesing-Harlaching\") (18).\n\nThere is no official division into districts. The number of districts is about 50, and if smaller units are counted as well, there are about 90 to 100 (see [map](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liste%20der%20Stadtteile%20Münchens#/media/Datei:Karte_der_Ortsbezeichnungen_in_München.png \"de:Liste der Stadtteile Münchens\")). The three largest districts are [Schwabing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schwabing \"Schwabing\") in the north (about 110,000 inhabitants), [Sendling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sendling \"Sendling\") in the southwest (about 100,000 inhabitants), and [Giesing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giesing \"Giesing\") in the south (about 80,000 inhabitants).[[114]](#cite_note-116)\n\n## Architecture\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=21 \"Edit section: Architecture\")]\n\nMain article: [Architecture of Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Munich \"Architecture of Munich\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Frauenkirche_Munich_-_View_from_Peterskirche_Tower2.jpg/250px-Frauenkirche_Munich_-_View_from_Peterskirche_Tower2.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Frauenkirche_Munich_-_View_from_Peterskirche_Tower2.jpg)\n\nThe [Frauenkirche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frauenkirche,_Munich \"Frauenkirche, Munich\"), consecrated in 1494, is a landmark and symbol of Munich.\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/GraphyArchy_-_Wikipedia_00383.jpg/250px-GraphyArchy_-_Wikipedia_00383.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:GraphyArchy_-_Wikipedia_00383.jpg)\n\n[Old Town Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Town_Hall,_Munich \"Old Town Hall, Munich\") and [Heiliggeistkirche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heilig-Geist-Kirche,_Munich \"Heilig-Geist-Kirche, Munich\") seen from [Viktualienmarkt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktualienmarkt \"Viktualienmarkt\")\n\n### Old Town\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=22 \"Edit section: Old Town\")]\n\nAt the centre of the old town is the [Marienplatz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marienplatz \"Marienplatz\") with the [Old Town Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Town_Hall,_Munich \"Old Town Hall, Munich\") and the [New Town Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Town_Hall,_Munich \"New Town Hall, Munich\"). Its tower contains the [Rathaus-Glockenspiel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rathaus-Glockenspiel \"Rathaus-Glockenspiel\"). The [Peterskirche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Peter's_Church,_Munich \"St. Peter's Church, Munich\") is the oldest church of the inner city. Nearby St. Peter, the Gothic hall-church [Heiliggeistkirche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heiliggeistkirche_(Munich) \"Heiliggeistkirche (Munich)\") was converted to baroque style from 1724 onwards and looks down upon the [Viktualienmarkt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktualienmarkt \"Viktualienmarkt\"). Three gates of the demolished medieval fortification survive; these are the [Isartor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isartor \"Isartor\"), the [Sendlinger Tor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sendlinger_Tor \"Sendlinger Tor\"), and the [Karlstor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karlstor \"Karlstor\"). The Karlstor leads up to the [Stachus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karlsplatz_(Stachus) \"Karlsplatz (Stachus)\"), a square dominated by the [Justizpalast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justizpalast_(Munich) \"Justizpalast (Munich)\") (Palace of Justice).\n\nThe [Frauenkirche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_Frauenkirche \"Munich Frauenkirche\") serves as the cathedral for the [Catholic Archdiocese of Munich and Freising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Munich_and_Freising \"Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Munich and Freising\"). The nearby [Michaelskirche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Michael's_Church,_Munich \"St. Michael's Church, Munich\") is the largest [renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance \"Renaissance\") church north of the Alps, while the [Theatinerkirche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatine_Church,_Munich \"Theatine Church, Munich\") is a [basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica \"Basilica\") in Italianate high baroque, which had a major influence on southern German [baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque \"Baroque\") architecture. Its dome dominates the [Odeonsplatz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odeonsplatz \"Odeonsplatz\").\n\n### Palaces and castles\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=23 \"Edit section: Palaces and castles\")]\n\nSchloss Nymphenburg ([Nymphenburg Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nymphenburg_Palace \"Nymphenburg Palace\"), construction started 1664) is a museum open to the public for tours.[[115]](#cite_note-Zen_2020_i476-117)[[116]](#cite_note-Lonely_Planet_2010_b951-118)\n\nThe smaller Schloss Fürstenried ([Fürstenried Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fürstenried_Palace \"Fürstenried Palace\"), construction 1715–1717) is used by the [Archdiocese of Munich and Freising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Munich_and_Freising \"Archdiocese of Munich and Freising\") as a conference location.[[117]](#cite_note-muenchen.de_2022_v258-119)\n\nSchloss Blutenburg ([Blutenburg Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blutenburg_Castle \"Blutenburg Castle\")) opened as a children's library in 2024,[[118]](#cite_note-Hordych_2024_l233-120) but visitors may tour the late-Gothic Blutenburg Castle Church built on the same grounds.[[119]](#cite_note-muenchen.de_2022_i705-121)\n\nThe large [Munich Residenz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_Residenz \"Munich Residenz\") complex on the edge of Munich's Old Town now ranks among Europe's most significant museums of interior decoration. Within the Residenz is the splendid [Cuvilliés Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuvilliés_Theatre \"Cuvilliés Theatre\") and next door is the [National Theatre Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Theatre_Munich \"National Theatre Munich\"). Among the mansions that still exist in Munich are the [Palais Porcia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais_Porcia \"Palais Porcia\"), the [Palais Preysing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais_Preysing \"Palais Preysing\"), the [Palais Holnstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais_Holnstein \"Palais Holnstein\") and the [Prinz-Carl-Palais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prinz-Carl-Palais \"Prinz-Carl-Palais\"). All mansions are situated close to the Residenz, so is the [Alter Hof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alter_Hof \"Alter Hof\"), the first residence of the [House of Wittelsbach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Wittelsbach \"House of Wittelsbach\").\n\n### Modernist architecture\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=24 \"Edit section: Modernist architecture\")]\n\nDespite Munich being the breeding ground for German [Jugendstil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jugendstil \"Jugendstil\"), starting with the architect [Martin Dülfer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Dülfer \"Martin Dülfer\"), Munich Jugendstil style was quickly submerged as historic trash. While the modernist architect [Theodor Fischer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodor_Fischer \"Theodor Fischer\") was based in Munich, his influence on Munich underwhelmed. Prior to 1914 the city of Munich was under-industrialized. During the [Weimar Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weimar_Republic \"Weimar Republic\"), the Munich establishment was hostile to [modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernism \"Modernism\"). The TUM professor [German Bestelmeyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Bestelmeyer \"German Bestelmeyer\") favored a conservative style, and [Jacobus Oud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobus_Oud \"Jacobus Oud\") was rejected for the post of city building chief. Modernist exceptions include a series of post offices by [Robert Vorhoelzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Vorhoelzer \"Robert Vorhoelzer\") built in the late 1920s and early 1930s. Examples of [avant-garde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avant-garde \"Avant-garde\") temporary constructions include the *Wohnmaschine* (*Housing Machine*) by [Robert Vorhoelzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Vorhoelzer \"Robert Vorhoelzer\"), as well as the *Flachdachhaus* (*Flat Roof House*) by [Fritz Norkauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritz_Norkauer?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Fritz Norkauer (page does not exist)\"). [Paul Schultze-Naumburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Schultze-Naumburg \"Paul Schultze-Naumburg\"), and the [Kampfbund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kampfbund \"Kampfbund\") enjoyed particular popularity.[[120]](#cite_note-122)\n\n### High rise buildings\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=25 \"Edit section: High rise buildings\")]\n\nSeveral high-rise buildings are clustered at the northern edge of Munich in the skyline, like the [HVB Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypo-Haus \"Hypo-Haus\"), the [Arabella High-Rise Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabella_High-Rise_Building \"Arabella High-Rise Building\"), the [Highlight Towers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highlight_Towers \"Highlight Towers\"), [Uptown Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uptown_Munich \"Uptown Munich\"), Münchner Tor and the [BMW Headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BMW_Headquarters \"BMW Headquarters\") next to the [Olympic Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympiapark_(Munich) \"Olympiapark (Munich)\"). Further high-rise buildings are located in the [Werksviertel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Werksviertel?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Werksviertel (page does not exist)\") [[de](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Werksviertel \"de:Werksviertel\")] in [Berg am Laim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berg_am_Laim \"Berg am Laim\").\n\n### Long-term residential development\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=26 \"Edit section: Long-term residential development\")]\n\nMunich is subject to a long-term residential development plan that is established by the city administration of Munich. The LaSie (\"Langfristige Siedlungsentwicklung\") was passed in 2011 in response to the acute housing crisis. LaSie is aligned with the strategic development plan passed for Munich in 1998 (\"Perspektive München\"). LaSie defines three priorities for the construction of residential housing in Munich. Existing [housing estates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_estate \"Housing estate\"), post-war low-density developments, and the suburban area are subject to [densification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Densification \"Densification\") (\"Nachverdichtung\"). Non-residential industrial areas are subject to conservation and will be turned into residential and mixed-use areas. On greenfield sites in the Munich periphery medium and large-scale housing estates are to be built so as to extend Munich's urban center.[[121]](#cite_note-123)\n\n## Parks\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Munich&action=edit&section=27 \"Edit section: Parks\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Englischer_Garten_from_Monopteros.JPG/250px-Englischer_Garten_from_Monopteros.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Englischer_Garten_from_Monopteros.JPG)\n\n*[Englischer Garten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Englischer_Garten \"Englischer Garten\")* with Munich skyline\n\n[Friedrich Ludwig von Sckell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Ludwig_von_Sckell \"Friedrich Ludwig von Sckell\") became famous for designing the [Englischer Garten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Englischer_Garten \"Englischer Garten\") between 1789 and 1807. Besides planning the first public garden in Europe, Sckell also redesigned Baroque gardens as landscape gardens, including the parks of [Nymphenburg Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nymphenburg_Palace \"Nymphenburg Palace\") and the [Botanischer Garten München-Nymphenburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botanischer_Garten_München-Nymphenburg \"Botanischer Garten München-Nymphenburg\").[[122]](#cite_note-124)\n\nOther large green spaces are the [Olympiapark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympiapark,_Munich \"Olympiapark, Munich\"), the [Westpark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westpark_(Munich) \"Westpark (Munich)\") and the [Ostpark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostpark_(Munich) \"Ostpark (Munich)\").
